The copy is plain, not encrypted: field encryption (M0-22) protects the sensitive columns, and the encrypted "Export backup" of blueprint 6.5 is a separate feature. | The desktop shell (M2) decides retention and the data directory layout, and the export feature is specified. |
| OQ-166 | What does "schema version" mean for readiness and for released-schema tests? | The version is the number of the last row in `schema_migrations`. Team readiness (blueprint 5, `/readyz`) should compare `currentVersion()` with `latestVersion` and fail on any difference, while startup on desktop only refuses a newer database (`refuseNewer()`). Blueprint 6.1 also asks for migrations to be applied "from every released schema" in CI; no schema has been released, so there is no fixture yet. | The first release exists (M0 exit); each released schema is then kept as a fixture database and the integration tests migrate from every one. |

## Dialer and trust store (M0-26)

Decisions the work item leaves open, taken in `libs/engine/net-dialer` and `libs/engine/net-truststore`. Each is easy to change there.

| ID | Question | Context | Closes when |
| ------ | -------- | ------- | ----------- |
| OQ-180 | How does a `NetworkError` become a Problem? | `net-*` may not import the kernel (blueprint 2.2), so they cannot throw `AppError`. `NetworkError` carries a contract `ErrorCode` (`TLS_UNTRUSTED`, `EGRESS_DENIED`, `BROKER_UNREACHABLE`, `VALIDATION_FAILED`), a safe `detail` and `retryable`, but `toProblem` maps only `AppError`, so today it would answer `INTERNAL`. Same root as OQ-116. | OQ-116 moves the error class into `@app/contracts` (or `toProblem` learns to read a `code`), and `NetworkError` is replaced or extended. |
| OQ-181 | Should the port carry the profile's TLS trust? | M0-23 landed `DialerPort` (`check`, `connect` returning a `Duplex`, `forwarder`) and `Dialer` extends it. The port's `DialTarget` has no `tls`, so a caller that holds only the port cannot ask for a verified connection; `DialTarget.tls` (a `TlsTrust`) and `dial()`, which also returns the handshake outcome, are net-dialer's own. | The connection manager (M0-27) needs verified connections through the port; then `tls` moves to the port as an opaque, port-typed value, or the manager depends on `Dialer`. |
| OQ-182 | Is allow-all the right default egress policy until M3-09? | The hook exists and is exercised (IP literals checked up front, one resolution, the connection uses the approved addresses, a policy that throws or answers anything but `allowed: true` denies). The default policy allows everything, because a desktop user dials their own network. The safety-first proposal denies cloud metadata addresses (169.254.169.254) even on desktop. | Maintainer decides whether the desktop default denies metadata and link-local addresses now, or waits for M3-09. |
| OQ-183 | Are the locations allowed to import `net` and `tls` right? | `eslint.config.mjs` now confines `net`, `node:net`, `tls` and `node:tls` (I-9; they were unconfined). Allowed: the two `net-*` projects, the adapters named in blueprint 2.3 (guessed, none exist yet), `transport` and `apps/engine` (listeners), `apps/cli` and `apps/desktop` (local IPC), `apps/conformance`, and test and tooling code (`libs/testkit`, `testing`, `tools`). | The adapters and apps exist and each location is confirmed, or narrowed to the connector files only ("adapter connectors"). |
| OQ-184 | Is "no leaf pinning" absolute? | DC-44 says never pin the leaf, so there is no fingerprint-match test and none of the profile can pin one. `fingerprint256` is in the diagnostics for a person to compare, not for the engine to enforce. Threat T16 mentions "profile pinning"; it is read here as the profile's own CAs. | Maintainer confirms, or asks for CA pinning (accept only one given issuer) as an option. |
| OQ-185 | The undici connector is checked against undici's documented contract, not against undici. | The `undici` package is not a dependency yet (the connector declares the option and callback types itself and offers HTTP/1.1 only, no ALPN). The specs drive the function directly with an HTTP exchange over the returned socket. | The management HTTP adapter (M0-24) did not use it: it dials with `Dialer.dial` from a `node:http` agent (OQ-410). The connector stays unused until `undici` is adopted (OQ-410) and runs inside a real `new Agent({ connect })`; then fix the types here if the contract drifted. |
| OQ-186 | Is the forwarder safe enough against other local users, especially on Windows? | A forwarder opens its upstream first, then listens for one client: a 0600 socket in a 0700 directory on Unix, a named pipe with a 128-bit random name on Windows, closed after the first client or 30 seconds. On Windows the default pipe DACL lets other local users open the pipe, and a second client is left waiting rather than refused. No credential is exposed (the client's own handshake carries them), but a local process could take the slot. Spike S-08 looks at an owner check. | S-08 settles the Windows check, or the forwarder is limited to tunnel routes (M1-22) where it is needed. |
| OQ-187 | Where do the test PKI and the lab integration test live? | The in-memory PKI (`@app/net-truststore/testing`, no key material on disk, no openssl) is used by both `net-*` projects but sits in the production library's `src/testing`, because `type:net` may not import `type:test` projects. The lab test (`net-dialer:it`) lives in `libs/testkit/src/it` for the same reason and is started by `libs/testkit/src/it/run-it.ts`, which exits 1 without a Docker daemon instead of skipping. | A shared test-PKI project exists, or the boundary rule lets `type:net` specs import `type:test`. |
| OQ-188 | `tls.setDefaultCACertificates` is process-wide and untested under Electron's BoringSSL. | `TrustStore.install()` replaces the default CAs for every TLS client in the process, including libraries that never see the Dialer; connections made through the Dialer do not depend on it (they pass an explicit secure context). The research asks whether `getCACertificates('system')` and `setDefaultCACertificates` work in Electron 44 (U-16). If the system store cannot be read, the store continues with bundled and organisation CAs and says so in `sources.systemError`, which fails closed for corporate CAs. | The desktop spike (M0-18/19) answers for each OS, and the UI shows `sources.systemError`. |
| OQ-189 | Are the connect defaults right? | Resolution, TCP connect and the TLS handshake share one 10 second timeout (`connectTimeoutMs`); TCP no-delay is on; the TLS floor is 1.2 (set in the secure context, not inherited from the process); keep-alive is left to the caller. | Maintainer confirms, or the connection manager (M0-23/27) needs other values. |
| OQ-190 | Should these decisions have an ADR? | The definition of done asks for one when a decision is taken. The choices here (resolve once and connect to the approved addresses; profile CAs concatenated onto bundled, system and organisation CAs; insecure only for exactly `true`; no leaf pinning) follow DC-43/44 and blueprint 7.5, so no new ADR was written, to avoid colliding with parallel work-item numbering. | Maintainer wants ADR-035, or confirms DC-43/44 are enough. |

## Ports and scheduler (M0-23)

Decisions the issue leaves open, taken in `libs/engine/ports` and `libs/engine/kernel/src/scheduler` so the ports could be typed and the scheduler model-tested. The adapters (M0-24, M0-26, M0-27) and the ActionExecutor (M0-29) must agree with them.

| ID | Question | Context | Closes when |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| OQ-170 | Where does `Principal` live? | `ConnectionManager` takes a `Principal`, and ports may import only contracts, pure and ports, so the type is declared in `@app/ports` (copied from core-interfaces.md). The ActionExecutor (M0-29, kernel) needs the same type in `ActionContext`; kernel can import it from ports, but the UI and the contract kit also need a principal shape (audit, `allowedActions`). | M0-29 or the audit work item needs the type outside the engine; then it moves to `@app/contracts` as a zod schema and ports re-export it. |
| OQ-171 | Are the settlement proofs right as built, and who may mint them? | A-9 asks for nominal proofs minted in `PublishLease` and the planner. Built: `unique symbol` brands the package does not export (compile time) and a module-private `WeakMap` set by `mint*` (run time, `assert*` in lease implementations). Changes against core-interfaces.md: the discriminator is `kind` (not `__proof`), and the AMQP 1.0 `stream-credit` literal is a fourth nominal proof (`StreamCreditProof`, minted by the stream consumer of an observe lease). Not built: a proof is not bound to one delivery tag and can be used more than once, so a `ConfirmedCopyProof` for message A can ack message B (binding needs the publish call to know the source tag, a change to `PublishLease.publish`); and `mint*` are plain exports, so nothing but review stops any code from minting. OQ-35 (the `tools/arch-tests` rule that bans `mint*` imports outside the publish lease, the planners and the stream consumer) is still open. | The peek/replay executors (M1) show whether a copy proof must name its source delivery, and `tools/arch-tests` lands with the minting rule. |
| OQ-172 | Are the scheduler defaults right? | Background pause 200 ms (up to 30 s), x2 after 3 consecutive overload signals, -50 ms per healthy answer; interactive 4, live 2 and background 1 in flight per broker, 6 together; 64 background requests across all brokers; queues of 256, 256 and 1,024 requests; a streamed body keeps its slot at most 120 s; a keyed GET is reused for 2 s (blueprint section 8 fixes only the 1 background, the 64, the 2 s and the AIMD idea). A refused request fails with `BUDGET_EXCEEDED` (retryable). | The inventory sweep and the first perf run (M1) measure real broker load, or the maintainer edits the table (`DEFAULT_AIMD` and the constructor defaults). |
| OQ-173 | How is the latency baseline learned? | The first full window (8 samples of 16) of background latencies sets the baseline; after that the baseline moves only with windows that were not slow (10 % per sample). A p50 above 2x the baseline counts as an overload signal. A broker that becomes permanently slower (a bigger cluster, a loaded node) therefore keeps the interval backed off until it reaches the maximum, and the baseline never adapts upward. Background streams (held slots) are not sampled. | The sweep (M1) runs against a real broker and shows whether the baseline needs a slow upward drift or a per-endpoint split. |
| OQ-174 | Is the single-flight and cache key safe and useful? | The key is the connection, the method and path, the sorted query and the identity of the schema object, so two call sites with separate but equal zod schemas do not share a call (safe, fewer hits). The key holds no credential or principal: today a connection has one stored broker identity, but per-user broker credentials (an OAuth token per team user) would let one user receive another's cached answer. Results are shared by reference for 2 s, so a caller that mutates one corrupts it for the next. | Per-user broker credentials are designed (M3), or a bug shows shared-result mutation; then the key gains the credential identity, or results are frozen. |
| OQ-175 | Is `PromScrapePort` right, and is `MgmtHttpPort` just the adapter? | I-8 names `MgmtHttpPort` and `PromScrapePort`; the issue lists only `MgmtReadPort` and `MgmtWritePort`. Built: the adapters implement `MgmtReadPort`, `MgmtWritePort` and a minimal `PromScrapePort` (`scrape({connectionId, node, path, query, lane}, signal)` returning chunks), and the composition root wraps each with `scheduleMgmtRead`, `scheduleMgmtWrite` and `schedulePromScrape`. Writes run in the `interactive` lane (changeable per wrapper) and are never cached or merged. The rule "only the wrappers are injected" is not enforced yet. | The Prometheus adapter implements its port (`adapter-mgmt-http` implements the other two, M0-24), and `tools/arch-tests` checks that raw adapters are injected nowhere but the wrapper. |
| OQ-176 | What belongs in `repos.ts`? | Only the async repository ports named in core-interfaces.md (`JobStore`, `ReplayJournal`) plus the `Window` and `WindowQuery` shapes that keep lists bounded (I-7). The settings, connection-profile and blob repository ports of M0-21 are still declared in `libs/engine/persistence/src/repos/ports.ts` (OQ-163) and were not moved here, to keep the two work items apart; the audit, plan and other repositories belong to the work items that need them. | The follow-up that OQ-163 asks for moves the M0-21 repository ports into `@app/ports` (`repos.ts`), and this file is extended to match. |
| OQ-177 | Does `DialerPort` need a typed route? | `connect` and `forwarder` take `route: unknown`, as in core-interfaces.md; the shape (direct, HTTP proxy, SOCKS, SSH tunnel) belongs to `net-dialer` (M0-26), which ports cannot import. M0-26 has landed with its own `Dialer` type (OQ-181), which adds `DialTarget.tls` and `dial()`; it does not yet extend `DialerPort`. `connect` returns a `Duplex`, not a `net.Socket`, because `node:net` is confined (OQ-183) and ports are outside the allowed locations; an adapter that needs socket-only members narrows it. | The follow-up of OQ-181: `Dialer` extends `DialerPort`, and the route type moves into contracts or ports. |
| OQ-178 | Are the new `trust:critical` paths in CODEOWNERS? | `libs/engine/ports` carries the `trust:critical` tag, and the scheduler sits in the kernel (only `libs/engine/kernel/src/actions/` is listed). CODEOWNERS is a protected path, so it was not edited. | A maintainer adds `/libs/engine/ports/` and `/libs/engine/kernel/src/scheduler/` to CODEOWNERS (see also OQ-65). |

## CSP spike (M0-20)

Decisions the spike (`spikes/csp`, [s-04-csp-trusted-types.md](architecture/s-04-csp-trusted-types.md)) leaves to a maintainer.

| ID | Question | Context | Closes when |
| ------ | -------- | ------- | ----------- |
| OQ-230 | Do AG Grid Community, Monaco and elkjs become catalog dependencies now, and who applies the protected-path edits? | The spike installs `ag-grid-community@36.2.0`, `monaco-editor@0.57.0` and `elkjs@0.12.0` into `tmp/csp-spike/deps` (npm, `--ignore-scripts`, `--before` three days), so nothing in `package.json`, the catalog or `tools/supply-chain/deps-allowlist.json` changed. Promoting them needs: three exact catalog entries; three allow-list entries (reason "UI grid / editor / graph layout", ref ADR-021 and M0-20); the licence gate (all MIT except elkjs `EPL-2.0 OR GPL-3.0-or-later`, a named exception, DC-51; transitive `dompurify` is `MPL-2.0 OR Apache-2.0`); and a look at the two low advisories on the `dompurify` 3.4.15 that Monaco 0.57.0 bundles (GHSA-p98j-92pf-mc4p, GHSA-6688-9rhm-gjv2, both on `IN_PLACE`; whether Monaco reaches that path is not checked). The `spikes/csp` entries in the `eslint.config.mjs` confinement table exist only for the harness. | M0-53 (ui-grid), M1-46 (Monaco wrapper) and M1-11 (graph) add the dependencies; the harness then reads them from the catalog and the `spikes/csp` confinement rows are removed. |
| OQ-231 | Accept the policy of the spike as the binding CSP, replacing blueprint 7.1? | The blueprint string does not give zero violations: Monaco needs `style-src-attr 'unsafe-inline'`. The spike also changes `base-uri` to `'self'` (Angular `<base href>`), drops `blob:` from `worker-src` and adds the `trusted-types` allow-list `app-worker-url app-monaco-html dompurify` (plus `angular` in the product shell). Trusted Types stay enforced. The ADR-021 addendum is marked proposed. Binding text lives in `docs/plan/amendments.md` and blueprint 7.1; M0-55 ("index.html carries the M0-20 CSP") and M0-59 (local web mode) should say the server or protocol handler adds the header to every response, workers included. | A maintainer accepts or amends the addendum and writes the amendment. |
| OQ-232 | Is `style-src-attr 'unsafe-inline'` acceptable for Monaco, or should the editor change? | Monaco renders each line through `innerHTML` with `style="top:..;height:.."`; a nonce cannot go on an attribute and the values vary, so only `'unsafe-inline'` works (about 80 reports per page load and overlapping lines without it). Risk: CSS injection through markup, bounded by the no-`innerHTML` lint rule, enforced Trusted Types and `img-src`. The alternative is CodeMirror 6, the research's first choice for the JSON editor (desktop-web-architecture.md); it was not tried here. | The maintainer accepts the risk, or M1-46 switches the editor and re-runs the matrix. |
| OQ-233 | Where do the load-order hooks live, and who checks them? | Monaco reads `MonacoEnvironment` and creates its Trusted Types policies when its modules evaluate, so the environment, the `<style>` nonce hook and the worker policy must run first. In the Angular build that is the `polyfills` entry of `apps/web`, not `libs/ui/editor`, which breaks the rule that a library owns its setup. Angular also needs `ngCspNonce` on the root and `optimization.styles.inlineCritical: false`; the stub shell has no styles, so inlined critical CSS (an un-nonced `<style>` and an `onload` handler) was argued, not exercised. | M0-51 (web shell) or M1-46 settles ownership; the `ui-editor` and `ui-grid` e2e specs then include the CSP check. |
| OQ-234 | Run the CSP matrix in CI, and re-run it inside Electron? | The matrix runs on Windows with Chrome and Edge 155 and takes about 90 s. CI runners have browsers, so a job on the three OSes is possible; the workflow was not touched. `app://` (custom scheme registration, module workers on a custom scheme, header per response in `protocol.handle`) is untested and belongs to M2-P15. The driver is dependency-free on purpose, so M2-P15 may reuse it or replace it with Playwright. | The Renovate lane needs the check for Monaco and AG Grid bumps, or M2-P15 starts. |
| OQ-235 | Enforce "no AG Grid string expressions" by lint? | A column with `valueGetter: 'data.size * 2'` makes AG Grid call `new Function`; under enforced Trusted Types that is a blocked `TrustedScript` sink, so it fails at runtime, never silently. A lint rule in `ui-grid` would fail it at review time. | M0-53 writes the grid adapter and decides. |

## ActionExecutor and PlanStore (M0-29)

Decisions the issue and the blueprint leave open, taken in `libs/engine/kernel/src/actions`, `libs/engine/ports/src/repos.ts`, `libs/engine/persistence` (migration `0002_plans`) and `tools/arch-tests`. Each is easy to change there; the audit and guardrail work (M0-30), the jobs (M0-31), the router (M0-32) and the action generator (M0-35) must agree with them. The executor is trust-critical (`libs/engine/kernel/src/actions/` is in CODEOWNERS).

| ID | Question | Context | Closes when |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| OQ-220 | Is the shape of the executor surface right? | It differs from core-interfaces.md in four ways. `run` and `execute` return tagged unions (`{kind: 'output' \| 'planRequired' \| 'job'}`) so an action output can never be mistaken for `{planRequired}` or `{jobId}`. `approve(planId, decision, ctx, note?)` takes the approver as `ctx.principal` (it needs mode and environment for the authorizer). Callers get a `PlanView` without the input (it may hold message bodies; the preview is the masked view), with `digest`, `onBehalfOfId` and a `failed` state added to the plan states. `ActionContext` is built by the executor from an `ExecutorContext` (an unset environment class becomes `prod`, the principal is copied and frozen). `GET plans/{id}` is `getPlan`. | The router (M0-32) and the UI (OQ-191) are written against it, or the shapes are changed. |
| OQ-221 | Are the error codes for the refusals right? | Unknown action: `NOT_FOUND`. Missing, malformed or reused idempotency key: `VALIDATION_FAILED` (a reuse is 409; no code names it). Plan owned by another principal, self-approval, an unattended approver, an MCP client executing: `FORBIDDEN_RBAC`. A plan that was already used (or whose run failed): `PLAN_STALE`. Awaiting approval or rejected: `APPROVAL_REQUIRED`. A failed audit-attempt write: `INTERNAL` (no `AUDIT_UNAVAILABLE` code exists). A payload over 8 MiB: `VALIDATION_FAILED` 413. Too many open plans: `BUDGET_EXCEEDED`. | The error catalogue (blueprint 3.6) gets `IDEMPOTENCY_REUSED` and `AUDIT_UNAVAILABLE` (a contracts change with the spec drift gate), or the choices are confirmed. |
| OQ-223 | Where do the plan and idempotency ports live? | The kernel owns the lifecycle (`PlanStore`) but may not import an adapter, and an adapter may not import the kernel, so `PlanRepository` and `IdempotencyRepository` are in `@app/ports` (`repos.ts`; the follow-up of OQ-176) and `persistence` implements them. `PlanRecord` stores the gates, warnings and preview as canonical JSON text (`gatesJson`, ...), so the store never re-encodes them and bigint and bytes survive. The test that joins the two halves is `tools/arch-tests/src/executor-persistence.spec.ts` (a `type:test` project may import both; the testkit uses `nodenext` resolution, which the extensionless imports of `persistence` do not satisfy). | The audit and receipt tables (M0-30) show whether a shared `repos` shape is wanted, and OQ-163 moves the other repository ports. |
| OQ-224 | Are the plan lifecycle rules right? | 5 minutes, or 24 hours awaiting approval; the last approval restarts a 5 minute window for the person who executes. One rejection vetoes. A plan runs once, and a run that failed or was denied after it started (audit attempt failed) consumes it (`failed`): the outcome of a failed mutation is unknown, so the same plan is not offered again; the idempotency key is consumed too. A plan stuck in `executing` after a crash is not reconciled here, nor by the job runner (M0-31 did not take it; it belongs to the start-up reconciliation of blueprint 1.5, OQ-318). `execute` re-runs the authorizer and the guardrail policy: a risk, approval count, typed phrase or consent set stricter than the plan's makes it `PLAN_STALE` ("plan again"). | The first mutating actions (M1) and the start-up reconciliation show whether a failed plan should be retryable or reconciled. |
| OQ-225 | Idempotency: refuse a reuse, or replay the first result? | The blueprint says "required and unused", so a key can be used once per principal and a retry with it is refused (409), even after a network failure; the caller plans again. Replaying a stored result would need the result (or its receipt id) kept per key. The key is checked early and claimed late, so a refused call does not burn it. The table has `purgeBefore`, but nothing schedules the retention yet. | Receipts (M0-30) exist and a replay can return the receipt, or the refusal is confirmed. Retention is scheduled by the composition root. |
| OQ-226 | Approvals are a JSON column of `plans`, not an `approvals` table. | Blueprint 6.2 lists `approvals` as a table. Each approval records the digest it approved, and the compare-and-set on `plans.version` keeps concurrent approvers consistent, so a column was enough. The audit chain (M0-30) needs an `approvalId`; it can be the plan id plus the approver. | M0-30 needs approval rows of its own (then a migration moves them), or the column is confirmed. |
| OQ-227 | Which parts are stand-ins until their work packages land? | `GuardrailPolicy` (`defaultGuardrails`: a typed phrase from the action's `confirm`, a plan for high and critical risk in `prod` and `staging`, a plan only for an unattended principal's mutation, no approvals; M0-30 replaces it with the prod lock, arming, freeze and quotas). `AuditSink` has no default: wiring none is a compile error, and `createMemoryAuditSink` is for tests. `ProfileResolver` (`noProfiles`: no fingerprint, no gates; `svc-capabilities` supplies the real one). `ReceiptSink` is optional (M0-30). `PayloadCipher` is a port with an AES-256-GCM test cipher (`createAesGcmTestCipher`, random key per process); `libs/engine/secrets` implements it with the vault's data key (M0-22, `vault.payloads`) but nothing wires it yet. Nothing here should be wired into a production composition root as it is. | M0-30 and `svc-capabilities` land; the composition root (M0-36) wires the real parts. |
| OQ-228 | Should authorisation come before the profile lookup? | Blueprint 5.1 orders the steps profile, gates, then authorise, and the rules file says the order is fixed, so the profile resolver (which may read the broker) runs for a caller who is later refused. Authorising first would avoid broker I/O for a forbidden caller and not reveal a gate's reason to one. | Maintainer confirms the order, or amends blueprint 5.1. |
| OQ-229 | Is the principal model right? | The authorizer is chosen by the composition root, never by the executor (ADR-001: it does not read the mode): `allowUiPrincipals(...)` for desktop and local, a role authorizer for the team server. Allow-all covers only a person at the UI (`surface ui`, kind `desktop-user` or `local-token`, attended, not acting for anyone); CLI, MCP, jobs and delegates need a grant (`area.verb`, with `*` on either side). A delegate holds only what it and the one it acts for both hold. An approver needs `plans.approve` and the action's permission, and may not be unattended or act for the requester. A plan may be executed by its requester, by the person the requester acted for (an MCP client plans, a person executes) and by a job running for the requester; an MCP client never executes. How API tokens map to roles is M3/M4. | The team RBAC (M3) and the MCP server (M4) define roles and token scopes. |
| OQ-240 | `tools/arch-tests` reads syntax with the TypeScript compiler API, not ts-morph. | AGENTS.md and the issue say "ts-morph architecture test". ts-morph is not in the catalog, and a new dependency needs the allow-list, which agents may not edit (OQ-151). The rules need only imports, identifiers, calls and type assertions, so `typescript` (already pinned) is enough. The rules go by name and err towards reporting. | A rule needs type information (then ts-morph, through the dependency policy), or the choice is confirmed. |
| OQ-241 | Is the funnel's scope right? | `FUNNEL_EXEMPT_PREFIXES` lists where write-capable names are at home: ports, kernel, persistence, `adapter-*`, `net-*`, `apps/engine`, the testkit, `testing` and `tools`. Everything else (services, transport, live, jobs, shared and UI code) must call `implementAction` or `defineJob` in any file that names one. `defineJob` (M0-31) exists; of `libs/engine/jobs` only `runner.ts` and `types.ts` are exempt (they build the write bundle a job body receives), and the rest of the project is checked like a service. The rule is syntactic and does not follow a re-export, `Amqp091Channel.publish` is inside the adapter and unchecked, and the "controller calls a service directly" part of blueprint 2.2 waits for the router (M0-32). The minting rule of A-9 (OQ-35, OQ-171) is included because it needs the same machinery: `mint*Proof` imports only in the ports, the two AMQP adapters, the planners and the testkit, and no type assertion to a proof type. | M0-32 lands and lists its files; the planners (M1) are placed. |
| OQ-242 | The plan-purity manifest is empty. | `registeredActions()` has one entry per `implementAction` call site, and a spec fails when a call site is not listed. There are no call sites yet (the only registered action, `meta.get`, is a query without a plan), so the AC is proven by the harness's own tests (`checkPlanPurity` with a plan that writes, one that writes and swallows the error, one that throws) and by the kernel's fixture actions. The action generator (M0-35) appends each new action's entry. | The first action lands (M0-35 golden path). |
| OQ-243 | Which defaults bound plan storage? | At most 100 open plans per principal (`BUDGET_EXCEEDED`), a sealed payload of at most 8 MiB, an idempotency key of 8 to 128 characters, an approval note of 500 characters, and the sweep (`PlanStore.sweep`) and the retention deletes (`purgeBefore`) are not scheduled by anything yet. | The composition root schedules the sweep and retention, and real plan sizes are measured (replay with edited bodies). |
| OQ-244 | Is the audit-outcome failure handling right? | If the outcome row cannot be written after the action ran, the result is still returned, flagged `auditOutcomeFailed: true`, and logged; the mutation cannot be undone. M0-30 may prefer to queue the row and retry. | M0-30 defines the audit sink's failure mode. |
| OQ-246 | Does the digest need a key? | The digest is plain SHA-256 over the bound fields, plus a copy sealed inside the AES-GCM payload. A database writer without the DEK cannot forge the sealed copy, so an edited row fails; a writer with the DEK could. Approvals are not in the digest (they accumulate), so each carries its own seal (AES-GCM over the plan id, approver, decision, digest and time, AAD `plans:approval:<plan>:<approver>`): a writer without the key cannot add, move or change one. The `state` and `expiresAt` columns are plain: a writer can lengthen a plan's life, but cannot make a purged plan run again or skip the approvals the executor re-checks from the sealed evidence. | M0-22 exists and the DEK model is final (an HMAC under a derived key is possible). |

## Audit chain, receipts and guardrails (M0-30)

Decisions taken in `libs/engine/kernel/src/{audit,receipts,guardrails}`, `libs/engine/ports/src/repos.ts`, `libs/engine/persistence` (migration `0003_audit_receipts`) and `libs/shared/contracts/src/registry/ops.ts`. They replace the stand-ins of OQ-227 (the guardrail policy, the receipt sink) and answer OQ-226 and OQ-244. The audit chain and the guardrails are trust-critical (`libs/engine/kernel/src/actions/` is in CODEOWNERS; `audit/` and `guardrails/` are new paths a maintainer should add).

| ID | Question | Context | Closes when |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| OQ-271 | Is the chain unkeyed SHA-256, with the database refusing UPDATE and DELETE, enough? | `hash = sha256("app.audit.v1\n" + canonical JSON of the other columns)`; `seq` (assigned by the kernel, 1, 2, 3, ...) and `prevHash` are inside it, `seq` is the primary key and `prev_hash` is unique, so a second writer loses the race instead of forking the chain. Triggers reject UPDATE and DELETE on both dialects (and TRUNCATE on Postgres): blueprint 6.1 says the same of a DML-only app role. Anyone who can drop the triggers and recompute every hash after the row they edited leaves a chain that verifies; only an anchor catches that (OQ-273). A keyed MAC would need the DEK of M0-22. The same triggers stop retention deletes (90 days desktop, 400 days team): retention must drop a partition or a maintenance step must remove the trigger first (M3-06). | M0-22 exists (a keyed hash is possible) and M3-06 writes retention and anchors. |
| OQ-272 | What is the audit sink's failure mode? (answers OQ-244) | An attempt row that cannot be written denies the action. An outcome row that cannot be written is reported on the result (`auditOutcomeFailed: true`), logged, and kept in a bounded in-memory queue (1,000 rows) that is written, in order, before the next row; while it cannot be written the next attempt is denied (fail closed). A row that does not fit the queue is counted (`stats().pendingDropped`) and logged as an error; it should stay 0 while the queue is sized right. The queue does not survive a crash, a write that succeeded but reported failure can leave a duplicate row (at least once), and a crash between the attempt and the outcome leaves an attempt with no outcome (the start-up reconciliation of blueprint 1.5 can close those, OQ-318). A failed run's outcome failure is only logged (the error is thrown, the flag has nowhere to go). | The start-up reconciliation closes orphan attempts, and the maintainer confirms at-least-once or wants a durable queue. |
| OQ-273 | Who stores the anchors, and who runs `audit verify`? | `AuditChain.verify()` reports the first seq at which the chain does not hold (`hash-mismatch`, `prev-mismatch`, `seq-gap`, `malformed`). It cannot see a truncated tail or a chain rewritten to its end, so `head()` gives what an external anchor stores and `verify({expect})` checks the chain against it (`truncated`, `anchor-mismatch`); `verify({from})` restarts after retention. The `audit_anchors` table, the export to WORM storage or a SIEM, the schedule, the `audit verify` CLI and the restore drill are M3-06. The desktop has no anchor store yet. | M3-06, and a decision whether the desktop anchors to a file next to the database. |
| OQ-274 | Is the prod lock the right shape? | Locked: every call that is not a query (commands, peeks, jobs, and a "query" that declares a mutating MCP tool) when the environment class is prod, unset, or not one of staging, test, dev, local. Open: `ops.arm` / `ops.disarm`. Arming is per person and per connection, lasts 15 minutes, is never extended (arming again starts a new 15 minutes from now), and lives in memory, so a restart locks again and a team server with several replicas would need a shared store. It is checked when a call is prepared and again when a plan is executed, so a window that closed in between refuses the plan with `GATE_BLOCKED`, not `PLAN_STALE`. A job or delegate acting for an armed person is not locked out. An AI client (MCP surface anywhere in the chain) has no mutations on prod even when the person it acts for is armed (blueprint 5.9 "mutations off"; 5.12 says plan-only, so this is the stricter reading). A call with no connection in its target cannot be armed and stays locked in prod, with no fix offered: the transport must send env `local` for local-only actions. | The transport (M0-32) and the first services decide env for local-only actions, and the maintainer confirms the MCP reading. |
| OQ-275 | Are refusals audited? | Only runs (attempt and outcome) leave audit rows. A call refused before it runs (`GATE_BLOCKED` by the lock, `FORBIDDEN_RBAC`, a stale plan) leaves none, so a probing client is visible only in the logs. T9 names "gaps"; a refusal is not a gap in what happened, but it is in who tried. | svc-audit (M3-06) decides whether denials get `phase: 'denied'` rows. |
| OQ-276 | Who may arm, and where do the actions live? | Only an attended principal on the UI or CLI surface that acts for no one, whatever its kind (not `mcp-client`, `job` or `system`); the policy and the action both check. The permission is `ops.arm` for both actions. There is no typed confirmation (a phrase would force a plan). The definitions are in the contract registry (`POST /connections/{connectionId}/arm` and `/disarm`, OpenAPI regenerated) and the implementations in `kernel/guardrails/ops-actions.ts`, because the lock they open lives in the kernel; they stay there after the action generator (M0-35) landed, because the lock they open lives in the kernel; `svc-ops` may move them. | M0-35 lands, and the UI work (M0-5x) shows whether arming needs a confirmation step. |
| OQ-277 | What does a receipt hold today? | The generic part: action, plan and digest, who (and for whom), correlation id, environment, risk, result and code, job id, the audit attempt seq, start and finish, and `detail` (masked JSON, at most 1 MiB, refused rather than cut) for the actions that have per-message results, anomalies and warnings (M1). Queries leave none. The audit outcome row names the receipt; nothing links back from a receipt to a hash, so a receipt edited later is not detected. A failed receipt write is flagged on the result (`receiptFailed: true`) and in the outcome row. Retention (90 days) is `purgeBefore`, not scheduled by anything yet. | The peek executor (M1) fills `detail`, and a maintainer decides whether the audit outcome should carry a receipt digest. |
| OQ-278 | Stored text is scrubbed in a bounded way, and `redactText` is not. | The URI pattern of `redactText` is quadratic on one long unbroken run of letters and digits, and so is the JWT pattern on a repeated `eyJ`; a 1 MiB string stalled a spec for minutes. Audit rows and receipts therefore go through `log/scrub.ts`: a string longer than 2,048 characters is replaced by a marker that says its length, the rest is redacted first and clipped after. `redact.ts` itself (logs) is unchanged, so a hostile log line still has that cost (I-5). | The patterns of `redact.ts` are made linear (M0-16 follow-up), or logs adopt `scrub.ts`. |
| OQ-279 | Which stand-ins and defaults went away? | `createActionExecutor` now requires `guardrails` and `receipts` (it had `defaultGuardrails`, which did not lock prod, and an optional receipt sink), `defaultGuardrails` is gone, `AuditSink.attempt` returns the row's seq, and `ReceiptSink.record` returns the id. A failed attempt write is still `INTERNAL` (OQ-221 asks for `AUDIT_UNAVAILABLE`). `createTestExecutor` wires the real policy over memory sinks (specs arm with `arming`) and `createWiredExecutor` wires the real chain and receipt store over memory repositories. `executeRawStatement` is exported from `@app/persistence` so tests and repair tools can run what Kysely cannot express (triggers); it takes SQL text, so nothing may pass it caller-supplied text. | M0-36 wires the composition root, and a maintainer decides whether the raw statement helper belongs behind a test-only entry. |

## AMQP 0-9-1 adapter (M0-27)

Decisions taken in `libs/engine/adapter-amqp091` so the five role-typed leases could be built on amqplib 2.2.0 and tested against the lab brokers. Each is easy to change there. The dual-library contract suite (M0-28), the ConnectionManager that pools these connections and the peek and replay executors (M1) must agree with them.

| ID | Question | Context | Closes when |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| OQ-210 | How does an `Amqp091Error` become a Problem? | The adapter may not import the kernel (blueprint 2.2), so it throws `Amqp091Error`: a contract `ErrorCode`, a stable adapter `reason` (`stream-get-refused`, `unknown-delivery-tag`, `barrier-failed`, ...), a safe `detail`, `retryable`, and the broker's close frame (`broker: {protocol: 'amqp091', classId, code, text}`) in the shape `ProblemSchema` already has. `toProblem` maps only `AppError`, so today it would answer `INTERNAL`. Same root as OQ-116 and OQ-180. A refused login is `UNAUTHENTICATED`, a certificate failure `TLS_UNTRUSTED`, 403, 404, 405 and 406 map to `FORBIDDEN_BROKER`, `NOT_FOUND`, `BROKER_RESOURCE_LOCKED` and `BROKER_PRECONDITION_FAILED`, every other reply code to `BROKER_ERROR`. | OQ-116 moves the error classes into `@app/contracts` (or `toProblem` reads a `code`), and the `reason` values get catalogue entries. |
| OQ-211 | Header types are inferred, not read from the wire. | amqplib decodes a field table into plain values and keeps the AMQP type only for timestamps and decimals: byte, short, int, long, their unsigned forms, float and double all arrive as JS numbers (a long above 2^53 loses precision before the adapter sees it). `TypedHeaderValue` wants a type, so integers are reported as `long` (the type RabbitMQ uses for `x-delivery-count` and `x-death.count`) and the rest as `double`; the IT shows a published `short` coming back as `long`. A table whose only keys are `!` and `value` is read as amqplib's own timestamp or decimal notation. Publishing is exact (`fromTypedHeaders` encodes the type asked for). A header named `__proto__` replaces the prototype of amqplib's decoded table; the adapter reads it back as a header. Nesting deeper than 32 levels is cut off with a marker string on the way in and refused on the way out. | The decoders (M1) say whether long-versus-int matters (it does for the dead-letter frameworks of `framework-failure-formats.md`), and the contract suite compares with @cloudamqp/amqp-client, which keeps the wire type. |
| OQ-212 | amqplib cannot take a socket, so it connects to the Dialer's forwarder. | `amqplib.connect()` calls `net.connect` or `tls.connect` itself, and its `exports` map forbids `amqplib/lib/connection` (the only place a socket could be passed). The adapter asks the Dialer for a `forwarder` (the egress policy and one DNS resolution run first, the upstream socket is already open), then hands amqplib that Unix socket or Windows named pipe as `path`; `net.connect({path})` ignores host and port, and the host it is given is `192.0.2.1` (RFC 5737) as a second line of defence. `connect.wiring.spec.ts` runs the real amqplib against a stand-in endpoint to prove it. Consequences: (1) for `amqps` the TLS handshake is amqplib's own over the local endpoint, configured from `TlsTrust.connectOptions` (profile CAs added to the defaults, the verified name, the insecure switch), so no `CertDiagnostics` come back from the Dialer and `info.tlsInsecure` is the only record; (2) OQ-186 applies to every AMQP connection on Windows (the pipe is reachable by other local users for the instant before amqplib connects); (3) a forwarder accepts one client and closes after 30 s, so amqplib's own recovery (reconnect with the same path) cannot be used, and the adapter does not use it. This answers OQ-181 for AMQP: `ConnectOptions.dialer` is `Pick<DialerPort, 'forwarder'>` and the TLS trust is a separate `TlsTrust`. | Upstream amqplib accepts a `socket` or `createConnection` option (an issue is worth opening), or the maintainer prefers the `createRequire` route to the internal `Connection` class, which keeps the Dialer's `CertDiagnostics` but pins to amqplib 2.2.0 internals. |
| OQ-213 | Is one publish in flight per lease enough, and what is a proof's `destination`? | With `mandatory` the broker sends `basic.return` and then the confirm, so with one publish at a time the return belongs to it without a correlation id; the cost is no pipelining (a replay of many messages pays one round trip each). A timed-out publish drops its confirm channel, so a late confirm cannot be taken for the next message's. The proof's `destination` is the routing key for the default exchange (the queue name) and `exchange/routingKey` otherwise. A `ConfirmedCopyProof` still names no source delivery (OQ-171). `props` is a whitelist (content type and encoding, correlation id, reply-to, message id, type, user id, app id, delivery mode, priority, expiration, timestamp, typed `headers`); anything else, `mandatory`, `CC` and `BCC` included, is refused, and untyped `headers` are refused. | Replay (M1) is measured and needs pipelining (then a per-message correlation, for example the message id echoed in the return), or the destination format is fixed by the quarantine and park planners. |
| OQ-214 | What is a 0-9-1 `control` lease? | The blueprint lists the role (metadata, "AMQP management channel", never holds messages) but `@app/ports` has no control lease type and `ConnectionManager` no `control()`. The adapter exports a local `ControlLease` with `checkQueue`, `checkExchange` and `release`, each call on its own throwaway channel (a 404, 403 or 405 closes only that channel). HTTP metadata stays in `adapter-mgmt-http`; the AMQP 1.0 management channel is M1-07. | The ConnectionManager work item decides whether `control` becomes a port type, or the passive declares move into the peek lease's `throwaway` only. |
| OQ-215 | On a quorum queue the qos barrier answers before the queue shows the nack. | Lab 4.3.6, three `basic.get`, one multiple nack with requeue, then the barrier: a classic queue showed all 3 messages ready at once, a quorum queue showed 0 ready and 3 consumers right after the barrier and all 3 ready 61 ms later (the return goes through the Raft log). So the barrier proves that the channel processed the nack and handed it to the queue, which is what DC-04 needs for a graceful close, but not that the queue has applied it; the `AC-2` integration test therefore asserts "ready at once" for classic and "ready within 10 s" for quorum, and prints the delay. This is the same uncertainty as `uncertainties.md` (a DOWN overtaking a pipelined nack under Raft leader changes) in a milder form. | The chaos cells of M0-28 and M1-14 measure the quorum case under leader transfer, and the peek executor decides whether it waits for the queue's ready count before it closes. Decided 2026-10-26 (M1-14): the executor waits on quorum queues, up to 10 s, for the ready count to be back where it was before the first `get`; otherwise the hold is an `incident` (`return-unconfirmed`). |
| OQ-216 | What does `release('healthy')` do with deliveries that are still held? | Peek: it returns them first (one multiple nack with requeue, then the barrier, then it closes the channel and the connection) and emits a `release-flush` event, because for a peek that is the only permitted way back (DC-04). Source: it refuses (`held-deliveries`) and stays usable, because a nack without a `SafeReturnProof` could drop a message on a finite-limit quorum queue; the job settles, or releases `poisoned` and accepts the broker's DOWN. Observe: it cancels and closes (tap copies only). `poisoned` never nacks: it closes at once. A channel the adapter gave up on after an unanswered `get` still settles and barriers (`lost` but not `gone`), so the watchdog can return what is held. | The peek and replay executors (M1) are written against it; the maintainer may prefer a throwing release for the peek too. |
| OQ-217 | Where does the queue type for the stream check come from, and what if it is stale? | `connectPeek` takes a `QueueTypeLookup` (the planner's preflight cache). A stream and an unknown type both make `get` throw `before anything is sent` (a `basic.get` on a stream is a connection-level 540 that returns every held delivery). A queue deleted and recreated as a stream within the cache's lifetime is not caught; the broker's 540 then closes the connection, and the lease reports it as `connection-closed`. | The preflight cache has a lifetime and the peek planner refreshes the type inside the hold budget; a conformance cell for `get` on a stream is recorded. |
| OQ-218 | Are the adapter defaults right? | Heartbeat 30 s (blueprint 5.3); handshake 10 s; 30 s for any `get`, qos, consume or passive declare (a timed-out reply abandons its channel); at most 1,000 held deliveries on a peek (the peek caps stop at 500), 10,000 prefetch on a consumer, 32 tap queues and 8 event subscribers per connection; a connection-name segment is 1 to 63 of `A-Za-z0-9._:@+=<>-`; amqplib's own `frameMax` (131,072) and the server's `channelMax`. Event subscribers are bounded and a throwing subscriber is ignored. amqplib's opt-in recovery is not used. Four throwaway channels (passive declares) at once per lease. amqplib reads a whole message into memory before the adapter sees it, so the byte cap of a peek (16 MiB) is the executor's; the lease bounds counts only. | The first perf run and the ConnectionManager (budgets per role) show whether the defaults need to move. |
| OQ-219 | What happens to held deliveries after a server-initiated cancel? | The consumer callback receives `null`; the lease passes `onDelivery(null)`, emits `server-cancel`, and keeps the tags of the deliveries it holds so the job may still try to settle them. Whether the broker accepts an ack or nack after the cancel is unknown and is the evidence cell of M0-28 (`server-cancel`); the IT only shows that the cancel is reported when the queue is deleted. | M0-28 recorded it for the queue-deleted trigger (OQ-261). |
| OQ-250 | Broker-backed tests are not part of CI yet, and `openssl` is needed for the TLS variant. | `pnpm nx run adapter-amqp091:it --versions=4.2.9,4.3.6` (25 tests: both versions in the plain variant, plus the TLS variant on the first version) lives in `libs/testkit/src/it` because a `type:adapter` project may not import `type:test` (OQ-187), and it needs a Docker daemon (it fails instead of skipping). Like `net-dialer:it` it has no job in `ci.yml` (only `persistence:it` does). On Windows the TLS variant needs `openssl` on the PATH (Git for Windows ships one in `usr\bin`); in PowerShell the version list must be quoted. The architecture test that bans `mint*` imports outside the publish lease, the planners and the stream consumer (OQ-35) has not landed: today `leases/publish.ts` is the only non-test file that imports `mintConfirmedCopyProof` and no non-test file imports another `mint*`. | A maintainer adds a Linux job for the lab ITs, and `tools/arch-tests` lands. |

## AMQP 0-9-1 contract suite (M0-28)

The dual-library suite of `libs/engine/adapter-amqp091/contract` (`pnpm nx run adapter-amqp091:contract --broker=all`): the adapter's leases run once on amqplib 2.2.0 and once on `@cloudamqp/amqp-client` 4.1.1 (a test-only shim behind the `Amqp091Driver` seam), against the five lab brokers. These are the decisions it needed.

| ID | Question | Context | Closes when |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| OQ-260 | How is SASL EXTERNAL covered, when only one library has it? | `@cloudamqp/amqp-client` 4.1.1 offers PLAIN only (fact sheet; upstream issue is U-25). The shim's `external()` returns a login and `connect` refuses it before anything is dialled (`cloudamqp-driver.unit.spec.ts`); the broker-backed EXTERNAL case is `it.skip` for that library with the reason in its name, which is the "expected skip" of the acceptance criterion. For amqplib the case only shows that asking for EXTERNAL from a broker that does not offer it fails with a typed error: the lab broker enables neither `rabbitmq_auth_mechanism_ssl` nor client certificates (`ssl_options.verify = verify_none`), so a successful EXTERNAL login is not proven by anything in this repository. | The TLS lab variant gets client certificates and the plugin (a lab change, so a maintainer decision), and the case becomes a real login; or the maintainer accepts that EXTERNAL is covered by the adapter's TLS tests only. |
| OQ-261 | What is the "cell" for a nack after a server-initiated cancel? | The scope names a cell; the plan puts the lab cells of U-17 in M1-06 (`CF-CANCEL-*`). M0-28 generated the lab-cell stub `CF-CANCEL-01` with `conformance-cell` (which also created the `apps/conformance` project) and made the contract case the live run: it writes `dist/contract-evidence/<broker>/server-cancel.json` for each library and queue type, and CI keeps that folder as an artifact. It is a candidate for `testing/lab-evidence/<broker>/CF-CANCEL-01.json`, which only CI's `conformance:record` and a human may write (I-14). There is no registry cell: `SettleActionSchema` has no `basic.cancel` entry, so a cell cannot be written without changing the schema. What the lab showed on 3.13.7, 4.0.9, 4.1.8, 4.2.9 and 4.3.6 (queue deleted under a consumer holding 3 deliveries, classic and quorum, both libraries): the cancel arrives as `onDelivery(null)` plus a `server-cancel` event naming the consumer, and a nack (requeue) and then an ack of held deliveries on the same channel are accepted (no channel error, the qos barrier answers); the same detection sees the 406 of a nack with an unknown tag. Only the queue-deleted trigger is exercised; the 4.3 quorum consumer timeout, the case U-17 is about, needs a broker configured with a short `consumer_timeout`. | M1-06 adds the consumer-timeout trigger and CI records the evidence; the semantics owner decides whether the settle matrix gains a `basic.cancel` action. `apps/conformance` needs a CODEOWNERS row (protected file, proposed in the work package report). |
| OQ-262 | What do the two libraries not agree on? | The shim translates to amqplib's shapes and adds no behaviour, so the adapter's seam stays amqplib-shaped (callbacks, `fields`/`properties`, `null` for a server cancel, error objects with a numeric `code`). Where the libraries differ the shim says so (top of `contract/cloudamqp-driver.ts`) and the suite asserts only what both can do: (1) header widths: a `byte`, `short` or `int` is written as an int32, a `long` (sent as a bigint) as an int64, and a double that is not integral as a float32 (`0.1` reads back as `0.10000000149011612`); decimals cannot be written; (2) `@cloudamqp/amqp-client` writes a plain integer from 2^31 up to 2^32 as an int32 that wraps (`3000000000` reads back as `-1294967296`): the shim avoids it by always sending typed `long` values as int64, but any other caller of that library would hit it; (3) no `serverProperties`, so `ConnectionInfo.serverVersion` is `undefined`; (4) the library has no cancel callback, so the shim reports a server cancel when the consumer is closed with an error while its channel is still open; (5) errors are translated from the library's message text (`channel N closed: ... (404)`), which is only as stable as that text. | The decision to swap libraries (the contract then tells what must change), or an upstream fix for (2). |
| OQ-263 | Where do the contract specs live, and what runs in CI? | The work package says `libs/engine/adapter-amqp091/contract/*.spec.ts`, but a `type:adapter` project may not import the testkit (OQ-187). The specs therefore take the broker as data: `libs/testkit/src/it/contract-broker.setup.ts` (a vitest global setup) starts one lab broker and passes its addresses in `CONTRACT_BROKER`, `libs/testkit/src/it/run-contract.ts` loops over the versions, and the one thing the specs need from the machine, `rabbitmqctl` (a broker-wide memory alarm for blocked/unblocked, closing every connection), goes through `docker exec` on the new `BrokerHandle.container`. Because those are broker-wide the contract files run one after the other (`fileParallelism: false`). `ci.yml` gains the job `amqp091-contract` (Linux, all five brokers, about a minute of tests per broker plus the image build); the other lab ITs of OQ-250 still have no job. The shim's pure translations run in `nx test` (`*.unit.spec.ts`); the broker-backed ones (`*.contract.spec.ts`) do not. | A maintainer moves the lab ITs of OQ-250 into the same job, or prefers the contract suite in `libs/testkit` like the other ITs. |

## Transport (M0-32)

Decisions the issue, the blueprint and the executor leave open, taken in `libs/engine/transport`. The transport is trust-critical (`/libs/engine/transport/` is in CODEOWNERS). Each is easy to change there; the live hub (M0-33), the tunnel (M0-34), the engine root (M0-36) and the web shell (M0-51) must agree with them.

| ID | Question | Context | Closes when |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| OQ-280 | Which error code does a transport refusal carry? | The catalogue (blueprint 3.6) has no code for "method not allowed", "unsupported media type", "body too large" or "request refused by a guard". The transport answers those with `VALIDATION_FAILED` and the real HTTP status (405, 415, 413, 400; the executor does the same for its 413, OQ-221), and Host, Origin, Sec-Fetch and carrier refusals with `FORBIDDEN_RBAC` 403, whose title says "Not permitted". A client that switches on `code` cannot tell a guard refusal from a missing permission. `detail` is fixed text and never repeats the request. | The catalogue gets codes (for example `METHOD_NOT_ALLOWED`, `UNSUPPORTED_MEDIA_TYPE`, `PAYLOAD_TOO_LARGE`, `REQUEST_REFUSED`; a contracts change with the spec drift gate), or the choice is confirmed. |
| OQ-281 | Does the 800-line kit cap leave room for M0-33 and M0-34? | `pnpm nx run transport:kit-loc` counts lines of code (blank lines and comment-only lines excluded; `*.spec.ts` and `src/testing/` not part of the kit) in `libs/engine/transport/src`. M0-32 uses 692 of 800, 144 of them in `static-ui.ts`. The blueprint sizes the port server alone at about 200 lines (3.4), and the WebSocket upgrade and tickets of M0-33 come on top, so the cap will not hold. The ADR lists router, Problem mapping, auth hooks, request checks, the inject tunnel and the spec emitters (those live in `@app/contracts`); it does not list the static UI. | A maintainer amends ADR-004 (a higher cap, or the static UI counted apart), or `static-ui.ts` and the live and port code move to their own libraries. Until then M0-33 or M0-34 fails the gate they are given. |
| OQ-282 | Who routes the plan, approval and job endpoints? | Only actions with an `http` binding become routes. `POST actions/{actionId}/plans`, `GET plans/{id}`, `POST plans/{id}/approvals`, `POST plans/{id}/execute` and `jobs/{id}` are not actions in the registry (OQ-191), so they are not routed here, although `ActionExecutor` has `plan`, `approve`, `execute` and `getPlan`. The router does emit the two links to them: `Location: /api/v1/plans/{id}` on a 428 and `Location: /api/v1/jobs/{jobId}` (with 202 and `{jobId}`) for a job. | Contracts declare the plan and execute routes (OQ-191) and the transport maps them to the executor, in a follow-up work item. |
| OQ-283 | Where does the environment class of a call come from? | The router passes no `env`, so the executor treats every call as `prod` (ADR-006) and plans every high or critical command. The class belongs to the connection profile of the target (`cid` in the path), which the router does not read and must not (it holds no port). Update (OQ-783): the router still sends no environment class and every call is still prod. Option 3 (resolve the class per target) was not taken; only the declared local scope was added, so an action that names a connection stays locked until that connection is armed. | The executor or `svc-capabilities` resolves the class from the target (M0-30 prod lock), or the router is given a resolver. |
| OQ-284 | Is "loopback names on any port" right for the native local mode? | Blueprint 7.2 says "exact Host allow-list"; 1.2 says any port for container mode. The policy accepts `127.0.0.1`, `localhost` and `[::1]` on any port in every mode, plus the listed `publicOrigins` exactly (no wildcard, no suffix). A DNS rebinding page has to use its own name, which never matches; the port adds nothing to that. An Origin must be this Host spelled as an origin, so another local site on another port cannot call the engine even with a token. | The maintainer accepts it, or wants the listening port pinned in native mode (the composition root would pass it). |
| OQ-285 | What must the MessagePort tunnel strip, beyond the headers in M0-34? | The guards run on injected requests too: Host must be a loopback name or `engine.internal`, and an `Origin` or `Sec-Fetch-Site` the renderer adds is checked like a browser's. The M0-34 text strips Authorization, Cookie, Host and `x-forwarded-*`; it must also drop `origin` and `sec-fetch-*` (or the guards skip injected requests, which weakens nothing a socket can do but removes a layer). A request that carries the carrier secret over a socket is refused whatever else it proves. | M0-34 is written against it, or the guards are scoped to socket requests. |
| OQ-286 | The carrier header is `x-app-carrier`. | The issue writes `x-<product>-carrier`; the product name is a placeholder (ADR-029) and the safety-first proposal already used `x-app-carrier`. `CARRIER_HEADER` is one constant, so the final name is one edit plus the tunnel. | ADR-029 settles the name. |
| OQ-287 | `GET /bootstrap.json` is public. | The page needs `{engineVersion, apiVersion, mode}` before it can know whether it has a token (OQ-192), so the file is served without one, behind the Host, Origin and Sec-Fetch guards. It tells a local process the engine version and mode, which `/healthz` will tell it anyway. The team-mode CSRF token has no source yet. | Maintainer confirms, or the page fetches it with the bearer token. |
| OQ-288 | What must `index.html` contain for the nonce, and is the S-04 policy the binding one? | The server replaces every `__CSP_NONCE__` in `index.html` (root file only) with the nonce of the response, so the build must write `<meta name="csp-nonce" content="__CSP_NONCE__">` and `ngCspNonce="__CSP_NONCE__"` on the root element, with `optimization.styles.inlineCritical: false` (S-04). The default policy is the S-04 line, sent on every response (API answers too, as S-04 asks for workers); `BuildAppOptions.csp` replaces it. Nothing here decides OQ-231. | M0-51 and M0-55 write the placeholders; OQ-231 is decided. |
| OQ-289 | Static files are served by about 60 lines of the kit, not by `@fastify/static` 10.1.6. | The blueprint names the plugin. It is a second dependency (with its own tree) for GET and HEAD of a few hundred files, and the path rules are the part that must be exact. The kit takes a strict segment alphabet (`[A-Za-z0-9_@+=~-]` and inner dots; no dotfile, no non-ASCII name), checks the real path against the real directory (links that leave it are 404), and has no ranges, ETag, compression or directory index. | A maintainer prefers the plugin (allow-list entry for `@fastify/static` and its tree, a confinement row), or the UI needs file names outside the alphabet. |
| OQ-291 | Are the transport limits right? | Body 1 MiB (`bodyLimit`; plan payloads of 8 MiB, OQ-221, will need a per-route limit), headers 16 KiB, `requestTimeout` 30 s, path parameter 1,024 characters, no `trustProxy`. There is no rate limit on failed authentication (a 256-bit token makes guessing moot, but a flood is cheap), no cap on connections, and no access log: the only log line is an unexpected failure, so no URL or query string is ever logged. The composition root owns the server-level timeouts and connection cap. | A perf or abuse run (M0-36, M0-61) shows what is needed. |
| OQ-292 | Executor validation text reaches the client. | `VALIDATION_FAILED` from the executor lists `path: message` per zod issue; for an unknown key that includes the key name the client sent (for example `Unrecognized key: "x"`). The answer is `application/problem+json` with `nosniff`, so it cannot run as markup, but it is client text echoed back. | Maintainer accepts it, or `describeIssues` stops naming keys. |

## Secrets and field crypto (M0-22)

| ID     | Question                                                                       | Context                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | Closes when                                                                                         |
| ------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| OQ-300 | Which key wraps does each delivery mode configure, and in what order?          | The library picks nothing: `createDataKeyVault({wraps})` takes them in order of preference and uses the first that is not unavailable. Proposed: container `[file-kek (Docker secret or file), passphrase]`, local web `[keyring, passphrase]`, desktop `[ctl-delivered]`, team `[file-kek]` until Vault/OpenBao Transit/KMS wraps exist. `requiresMasterPassword()` is also true for the container's UI passphrase: the UI wording should not promise an OS master password there. M0-36 wired desktop `[ctl-delivered]` and local `[file KEK when --kek-file is given, keyring, passphrase]`; team waits for M3. | The UI copy for the passphrase prompt is decided and the team wraps exist (M3).                 |
| OQ-301 | How does the native keyring binding reach the engine?                          | `@napi-rs/keyring` is not imported: `KeyringBackend` is injected, so this library has no native dependency. The binding needs a catalog entry, an allow-list entry, a `pnpm-workspace.yaml` build allow-list entry (`allowBuilds`, a protected supply-chain key) and a confinement row, all human-only. Something must also report `basic_text` or `none` on Linux: the keyring library does not, Electron's `safeStorage.getSelectedStorageBackend` does. M0-36 injects a backend that reports `none`, so local mode falls to the passphrase (OQ-351).  | A maintainer decides where the binding lives (local mode, M2-P04 desktop) and applies the supply-chain changes. |
| OQ-302 | Is scrypt N=2^17 with a 12-character minimum the passphrase policy?            | The shipped KDF is scrypt (blueprint 6.4); argon2id depends on whether `crypto.argon2` works under BoringSSL (spike S-07). The wrap stores `kdf` and the cost, so a second KDF is a new value, not a migration. Wrong passphrases are not rate limited beyond the cost (about 0.3 s, one at a time); the failed-auth limit is the transport's (OQ-291). The 12-character minimum is a guess.                                | S-07 reports; the maintainer confirms the minimum and where failed unlocks are throttled.           |
| OQ-303 | Who rotates a data key, and what stops a key being used too often?             | Rotation and `rewrap` are not in M0-22. The port has `retire` and the vault opens retired keys, so a rotation is: insert a new active key, retire the old one, and re-wrap with `rewrap` (apps/cli). Random 96-bit IVs are safe to about 2^32 seals per key (NIST SP 800-38D); there is no counter, so a busy key is not detected. A single value is capped at 16 MiB (`MAX_PLAINTEXT_BYTES`), larger bodies are chunked by their owner (M1-09). | The `rewrap` work package is written; it decides rotation triggers and whether to count seals.     |
| OQ-304 | Should the vault's error codes join the contract error catalogue?              | `SecretsError` codes (`VAULT_LOCKED`, `UNWRAP_FAILED`, `KEK_INVALID`, `KEK_MISMATCH`, `WRAP_UNAVAILABLE`, `WRAP_NOT_CONFIGURED`, `WEAK_PASSPHRASE`, `DECRYPT_FAILED`, `KEY_NOT_FOUND`, `INVALID_INPUT`) are the library's own; `ErrorCode` in `@app/contracts` has none of them, so the composition root must map them to a Problem (probably `INTERNAL`, with a status the UI can act on for a locked vault). | The maintainer decides whether `ErrorCode` grows; M0-36 maps the rest.                              |
| OQ-306 | Which action stores and clears a credential, and what does it return?          | `secretInput()` and `SecretStatusSchema` (`isSet`, `updatedAt`) are the write-only pair, and `checkRegistry` rejects a secret in an output, a topic, a path or a GET. The connection actions of M0-44 are the first to use them (create, update and the tests take secrets; nothing returns one) and their specs scan for canary secrets in responses, audit rows, receipts and logs. The end-to-end canary scan of I-10 across the whole engine does not exist yet. | The canary scan lands with the E2E suite.        |

## Live hub (M0-33)

Decisions the issue and the blueprint leave open, taken in `libs/engine/live` (the hub) and `libs/engine/transport/src/ws` (the upgrade). Each is easy to change there; the MessagePort tunnel (M0-34), the engine root (M0-36) and the topic services must agree with them. OQ-281 (the transport size cap) is not decided here.

| ID | Question | Context | Closes when |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| OQ-330 | How is a subscription authorised? | `LiveHubOptions.canSubscribe(principal, permission)` is a synchronous predicate, because a `sub` is answered in order by the session state machine. The kernel `Authorizer` is asynchronous and wants an action id, a target and an environment class, which a topic does not have. The composition root passes a predicate built from the same role table; there is no default, so a hub without one cannot be built. | M0-36 wires the predicate, or `Authorizer` gains a topic request and the hub awaits it. |
| OQ-331 | Why is the ticket store in `libs/engine/live` and not in `transport/src/ws/tickets.ts`, as the issue lists? | The transport kit was at 692 of its 800 lines (ADR-004) and the upgrade, the ticket route and the shared admission took it to 828 with the store in it. The store (`createTicketStore`, 38 lines) moved to `@app/live`; the transport keeps the route, the guards and the redeem, and stays at 790. The store is single-use and principal-bound logic outside a `trust:critical` project. | A maintainer adds `/libs/engine/live/src/tickets.ts` to CODEOWNERS, or amends ADR-004, or the transport kit is split. |
| OQ-332 | How does the engine notice a half-open WebSocket? | The hub parks a session when the transport reports `close`. A peer that vanished without a FIN stays attached until TCP gives up, and a `hello{resume}` meanwhile finds no parked session and starts a new one (the old one is dropped when its socket finally closes, and its sources stop then). `ws` has ping and pong control frames and the protocol has `ping`, but no interval or rule is chosen. See OQ-199 for the client side. | A heartbeat interval is chosen (blueprint 3.5 or an ADR) and the upgrade terminates sockets that miss it. |
| OQ-333 | Are published items checked against `TopicDef.item`? | `LiveHubOptions.validateItems` (default off) parses every published item with the topic schema. Off, a producer that breaks its own contract reaches the client; on, every item is parsed once more on the event loop (I-5). | The first real topic is measured, or the default is on for test and dev builds and off in release builds. |
| OQ-334 | Are the hub limits right? | Not in the blueprint, so chosen: 64 live connections per engine, 64 KiB per client frame (`hello`, `sub` and `credit` are tiny; `ws` `maxPayload` and the hub both enforce it), 1024 outstanding tickets (oldest forgotten), and a transport that holds more than `outboxBytes` (8 MiB) of unread output is closed with 1013 and its session parked. Close codes: 1008 protocol, 1009 too big, 1003 binary frame, 1013 full or slow, 1001 drain. | The team server (M3) is sized, or the numbers are confirmed. |
| OQ-335 | Where does `sampleEvery` of a `sampled` topic come from? | `TopicDef` has no such field, so the hub leaves it unset and the outbox keeps every item (n = 1). OQ-126 says deciding n from the tap rate is the hub's job; it is not built here. | The tap topics (M1) show the rate to sample, and `TopicDef` or the source gains the field. |
| OQ-336 | What does a refused handshake answer with? | `ws` takes the socket before Fastify, so the refusal is written by hand: a bare status line (`403` for a guard, `401` for a missing, reused, expired or foreign ticket, `404` for another path) with `connection: close` and no body. A Problem body is possible but a browser cannot read it from a failed `WebSocket` anyway. The UI treats any failed open as a network error (`HttpWsTransport`). | The UI needs the reason (for example to tell an expired ticket from a forbidden Origin), or the refusal gets a `problem+json` body. |
| OQ-337 | Is `POST /api/v1/live/tickets` an action? | It is a hand-registered private route of the transport, not a registry action, so the OpenAPI emitter does not list it and the I-2 funnel does not see it. It reads no broker and writes nothing but an in-memory ticket. Making it a `query` action would put it in the spec and the audit trail; the executor has no per-request state a ticket could be bound to except the principal. | The OpenAPI gate (`contracts:api-docs-check`) is made to cover hand-registered routes, or the ticket becomes an action. |
| OQ-338 | May a resume steal a session whose old socket is still attached? | `hello{resume}` claims only a parked session of the same principal id. While the old socket is attached (OQ-332) the claim fails and the client gets a fresh session. A session id is a 122-bit random UUID, but in team mode one person may hold several tabs under one principal id, so the id is what keeps a tab from resuming another tab's session. | OQ-332 is closed, then decide whether a resume may detach a live socket of the same principal. |

## Jobs and drain (M0-31)

Decisions the issue and the blueprint leave open, taken in `libs/engine/jobs`, `libs/engine/kernel/src/drain`, `libs/engine/ports/src/repos.ts` (the JobStore port), `libs/engine/persistence` (migration `0005_jobs`) and `tools/arch-tests`. The jobs library is trust-critical.

| ID     | Question                                                                 | Context                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | Closes when                                                                                      |
| ------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| OQ-310 | Where do the SQL job stores live?                                        | The issue names `libs/engine/jobs/src/{store-sqlite,store-pg}.ts`. `kysely`, `pg` and `node:sqlite` are confined to `libs/engine/persistence` (I-11) and `jobs` is `type:kernel`, which may not import an adapter, so the one Kysely store for both dialects is `persistence/src/repos/jobs.ts` (`FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` and row locks on Postgres only). The contract both stores and the in-memory store pass is `jobStoreContract` in `@app/ports/testing` (a new subpath, like `@app/net-truststore/testing`), and `pnpm nx run jobs:it` runs the job-store tests of `persistence:it` (`--filter="job store"`). `tools/arch-tests` joins the runner and the SQL store. | The maintainer confirms the placement, or wants one file per dialect inside persistence.          |
| OQ-311 | Is the reshaped JobStore port right?                                     | It differs from core-interfaces.md, which nothing used yet: a `JobLease` (job, owner, attempt) fences every write of a running job, so a runner that lost it cannot write even under the same owner name; `create` is idempotent per requester and key and returns the stored record; `setState` is replaced by `finish` (fenced, ends an attempt) and `transition` (compare-and-set, never a running job); there are `interruptRunning`, `list` (a window), `events` and `purgeFinishedBefore`; input, checkpoint and result are sealed bytes the store never reads. `JobDef.onDrain` gets its deadline as a duration, `drain` returns one entry per job, and `submit` takes the requester and derives the job principal. | The first service module that submits a job (M1) shows what is missing.                           |
| OQ-312 | Is the DrainCoordinator's shape right?                                   | core-interfaces.md has `DrainPhase {name, budgetMs, run(deadline)}` and `EngineModule.drainPhases()`. Several modules contribute to one phase and the blueprint fixes the budgets, so the coordinator owns them (10, 600, 800, 300 and 200 ms for 2 s, scaled to `totalMs`) and a contribution is `DrainPhaseSpec {name, run({reason, deadline, budgetMs}), reasons?}`; contributions to one phase start together and share its deadline. A phase that overruns is cut off and reported, but keeps running in the background, so `flush` can close the database while a timed-out peek holder is still nacking. A second `drain` while one runs returns the running one: a second SIGTERM does not restart it, and an `emergency` during a slow `quit` does not shorten it. | `EngineModule.drainPhases` is written (M0-36), or the maintainer wants a shortening escalation.   |
| OQ-313 | Is 100 ms the right reserve for writing a job's final state?             | The jobs phase has 800 ms; `onDrain` gets the phase budget minus `settleReserveMs` (100 ms), so the last write of `paused-interrupted` is not racing the phase deadline. If the database is too slow to take that write before `flush` closes it, the job stays `running` in the store and the next start interrupts it (`engine-restart`, or `lease-expired` on a shared database): nothing is lost, but the state reason is less precise. The unit is a guess.                                                                                                                                  | The first long-running job (M1 replay or move) measures how long a checkpoint and a release take. |
| OQ-314 | Which roles does a resumed job run with?                                 | A job runs as `job:<id>` (kind `job`, unattended) acting for the requester, with a copy of the requester's roles taken when it was submitted and sealed with the input. A role revoked afterwards still applies when the job resumes days later; `preflight` is the hook that can re-check, and a job body reaches the ports through `ctx.executorWrite` directly, not through the executor's authorizer.                                                                                                                                                                                                | The first job that writes to a broker (M1) settles whether resume re-resolves the principal.      |
| OQ-315 | Who audits a job's outcome, and what is `planHash` for a plan-less start? | The runner records every state change in `job_events` (fixed reasons and error codes, never messages) and calls `onState`; it writes no audit row or receipt when a job ends, interrupted or not, although the audit schema has a `jobId` column (I-13). `submit` needs a sha256 `planHash` for every job: an action with `planning: none` that starts a job passes `hashValue(input)`.                                                                                                                                                                                                             | The first service module that starts a job (M1) wires `onState` to the audit chain and receipts.  |
| OQ-316 | What resumes by itself, and in which mode?                               | Options, not mode branches (ADR-001): `recovery` (`interrupt-all-running` for desktop and local, which hold the data-dir lock; `interrupt-expired` on a shared database) and `autoResume` (`resumable` queues interrupted jobs that declared themselves resumable once their `preflight` allows it: the team server; `never` otherwise). `awaiting-credentials` is never resumed by the runner, and `maxAttempts` (5) fails a job that keeps being claimed (one that kills the engine). The number is a guess.                                                                                          | The first resumable job tests the 5. M0-36 mapped desktop and local to `interrupt-all-running` and `never` (under the data-dir lock); team waits for M3.                         |
